The issue describes a problem with **markdown list formatting** where:
- **Expected format**: Second line should have proper indentation (`>       item`)
- **Actual format**: Second line has **extra whitespace** (`>       item` with too much leading space)
- **Context**: This occurs in "maxed nested lists" - deeply nested markdown structures at maximum nesting depth

### 1.4 Steps to Reproduce
1. Create a deeply nested markdown structure (3-level nesting with blockquotes and lists)
2. Process it through pymarkdown
3. Check the second line indentation of list items
4. Compare expected vs actual output

### 1.5 Best Practices
- Make minimal changes to fix the specific indentation issue
- Ensure existing functionality isn't broken
- Focus on the whitespace calculation logic for nested structures
- Test both ordered and unordered list scenarios

    25	[linting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lint_%28software%29)
    26	is accomplished successfully, the rules engine that powers the linter
    27	uses a Markdown parser that is both
    28	[GitHub Flavored Markdown](https://github.github.com/gfm/)
    29	compliant and
    30	[CommonMark](https://spec.commonmark.org/)
    31	compliant.  The rules provided in the base application can be easily extended
    32	by writing new plugins and importing them into the rules engine through simple
    33	configuration options.
    34	
    35	The PyMarkdown project has the following advantages:
    36	
    37	- Consistency
    38	  - This project can examine multiple files and directories with one invocation,
    39	    ensuring that all detected Markdown files adhere to the same guidelines.
    40	- Portable
    41	  - The linter runs on any system running Python 3.8 or later, with no modifications.
    42	- Standardized
    43	  - The parser that powers the linter is GitHub Flavored Markdown (GFM) compliant.
    44	    Due to that foundation, the parser does not guess how some parsers may handle
    45	    a given situation, as it has a clear set of rules to follow.
    46	- Accurate
    47	  - The parser passes all GFM conformance tests and CommonMark conformance tests.  In
    48	    test scenarios that were not present in either set of tests, the CommonMark
    49	    0.29.2 release was used to determine the correct parsing.
    50	- Flexible

    def verify_markdown_roundtrip(source_markdown, actual_tokens):
        """
        Verify that we can use the information in the tokens to do a round trip back
        to the original Markdown that created the token.
        """

        if "\t" in source_markdown:
            new_source = []
            split_source = source_markdown.split(ParserHelper.newline_character)
            for next_line in split_source:
                if "\t" in next_line:
                    next_line = ParserHelper.detabify_string(next_line)
                new_source.append(next_line)
            source_markdown = ParserHelper.newline_character.join(new_source)

        transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
        original_markdown = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)

        print(
            "".join(
                [
                    "\n-=-=-\nExpected\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\nActual\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\n",
                ]
            )
        )
        diff = difflib.ndiff(source_markdown, original_markdown)
        diff_values = "".join(
            [
                "\n-=-=-n",
                ParserHelper.newline_character.join(list(diff)),
                "\n-=-=-expected\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-actual\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-\n",
            ]
        )

            source_markdown == original_markdown
        ), f"Markdown strings are not equal.{diff_values}"

    @pytest.mark.gfm
    def test_nested_three_block_max_block_max_unordered_max_no_bq1():
        """
        Verify that a nesting of block quote, block quote, unordered list, with

    def verify_markdown_roundtrip(source_markdown, actual_tokens):
        """
        Verify that we can use the information in the tokens to do a round trip back
        to the original Markdown that created the token.
        """

        if "\t" in source_markdown:
            new_source = []
            split_source = source_markdown.split(ParserHelper.newline_character)
            for next_line in split_source:
                if "\t" in next_line:
                    next_line = ParserHelper.detabify_string(next_line)
                new_source.append(next_line)
            source_markdown = ParserHelper.newline_character.join(new_source)

        transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
        original_markdown = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)

        print(
            "".join(
                [
                    "\n-=-=-\nExpected\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\nActual\n-=-=-\n-->",
                    ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                    "<--\n-=-=-\n",
                ]
            )
        )
        diff = difflib.ndiff(source_markdown, original_markdown)
        diff_values = "".join(
            [
                "\n-=-=-n",
                ParserHelper.newline_character.join(list(diff)),
                "\n-=-=-expected\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(source_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-actual\n-->",
                ParserHelper.make_value_visible(original_markdown),
                "<--\n-=-=-\n",
            ]
        )

>       assert (
            source_markdown == original_markdown
        ), f"Markdown strings are not equal.{diff_values}"

1. In `__apply_primary_transformation` (lines 448-450): `container_line = split_leading_spaces[last_container_token_index] + container_line`

TEST CREATION: Creating a Minimal Reproduction Script<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/jackdewinter__pymarkdown__0.9/reproduce_issue.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>create</arg_value><arg_key>file_text</arg_key><arg_value>#!/usr/bin/env python3

"""
Script to reproduce the nested list whitespace issue.
"""

from pymarkdown.tokenized_markdown import TokenizedMarkdown
from test.transform_to_markdown import TransformToMarkdown

def test_ordered_case():
    """Test the ordered list case."""
    print("=== Testing Ordered List Case ===")
    
    source_markdown = """   >    >    1. list
        >       item"""
    
    print("Source markdown:")
    print(repr(source_markdown))
    
    # Parse to tokens
    tokenizer = TokenizedMarkdown()
    actual_tokens = tokenizer.transform(source_markdown)
    
    print("\nTokens:")
    for token in actual_tokens:
        print(f"  {token}")
    
    # Transform back to markdown
    transformer = TransformToMarkdown()
    result = transformer.transform(actual_tokens)
    
    print("\nResult:")
    print(repr(result))
    
    print("\nComparison:")
    print("Expected:", repr(source_markdown))
    print("Actual:  ", repr(result))
    print("Match:", source_markdown == result)
    
    return source_markdown == result

    print("Actual:  ", repr(result))
    print("Match:", source_markdown == result)

def init_tokenizer():
    """Initialize tokenizer with proper configuration."""
    tokenizer = TokenizedMarkdown()
    test_properties = ApplicationProperties()
    extension_manager = ExtensionManager()
    extension_manager.initialize(None, test_properties)
    extension_manager.apply_configuration()
    tokenizer.apply_configuration(test_properties, extension_manager)
    return tokenizer

1. **Line processing**: `container_line>>21-41:>:        >       item:<` (correct - 8 spaces)
2. **First addition**: `-->container_line>        >       item` (still correct)
3. **Second addition**: `-->container_line>                >       item` (wrong - now 16 spaces!)

The issue is in the `__adjust_for_list` method where it adds the leading spaces even though they were already added in `__apply_primary_transformation`.

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS: Clear Problem Statement

### 5.1 What the problem is:
In the `TransformToMarkdown` class, when processing nested block quotes with lists, the container line's leading spaces are being added twice:
1. First in `__apply_primary_transformation` method (lines 448-450)
2. Second in `__adjust_for_list` method (lines 521-523)

### 5.2 Where the problem is located:
File: `/workspace/jackdewinter__pymarkdown__0.9/test/transform_to_markdown.py`
Methods: `__apply_primary_transformation` and `__adjust_for_list`

### 5.3 How the test reproduces the issue:
The test creates a deeply nested structure: `block-quote -> block-quote -> list` where the second line of the list item has no block quote markers but needs proper indentation. The transformation logic incorrectly doubles the leading whitespace.

### 5.4 Best practices for the fix:
- Make minimal changes to avoid breaking other functionality
- Ensure the condition checking prevents double application of leading spaces
- Preserve existing behavior for other cases
- The fix should be in the logic that determines when to apply leading spaces

### 5.5 How to fix the problem:
Looking at the debug trace, I can see that:
- `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is `True` when the issue occurs
- The condition on line 510 checks `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` OR line number difference
- When `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is `True`, we should NOT apply additional leading spaces in `__adjust_for_list`

The fix is to modify the condition in `__adjust_for_list` to prevent applying leading spaces when they've already been applied.

## Phase 6. FIX IMPLEMENTATION<tool_call>think<arg_key>thought</arg_key><arg_value>Looking at the code more carefully:

In `__apply_primary_transformation`, when `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is set to True, it means leading spaces were already applied to the container line.

Then in `__adjust_for_list`, there's a condition:
```python
if (
    applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line
    or token_stack[-1].line_number != previous_token.line_number
):
```

This means if `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is True, it will still apply the leading spaces again, causing the duplication.

The fix should be to NOT apply leading spaces in `__adjust_for_list` when they were already applied in `__apply_primary_transformation`. So the condition should be changed to only apply when `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is False AND the line numbers are different.

Actually, let me re-read the logic more carefully...

Looking at line 510-512:
```python
if (
    applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line
    or token_stack[-1].line_number != previous_token.line_number
):
```

This says: Apply leading spaces if EITHER we already applied them OR the line numbers are different. That doesn't make sense - if we already applied them, we shouldn't apply them again.

The logic should probably be: Apply leading spaces if we haven't applied them yet AND the line numbers are different (meaning we're on a continuation line).

So the fix should change the condition from:
`applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line or ...`

to:
`not applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line and ...`</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Your thought has been logged.</tool_response><|assistant|></think><tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/jackdewinter__pymarkdown__0.9/test/transform_to_markdown.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>view</arg_value><arg_key>view_range</arg_key><arg_value>[508, 515]</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Here's the result of running `cat -n` on /workspace/jackdewinter__pymarkdown__0.9/test/transform_to_markdown.py:

- When `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is `True`, it means spaces were already applied
- But the code adds spaces when this flag is `True` - this is backwards!

Let me look at how this flag is calculated:

```python

    did_move_ahead and current_changed_record[0]
)
```

So `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is `True` when we DID NOT move ahead OR when `current_changed_record[0]` is False.

The name is confusing. Let me understand what this really means by looking at the usage.

Actually, let me step back and think about this logically. The variable name suggests "we already applied leading spaces", but the logic is doing the opposite - it's adding spaces when this flag is true.

This is the bug! The logic is backwards. When `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` is `True`, it should mean "spaces were already applied", so we should NOT apply them again. But the code is doing the opposite.

However, I need to be careful. This might be working correctly in other cases. Let me check if changing this would break other things.

Actually, let me first understand what the intention is. Let me look at the condition where spaces are added:

```python
if applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line:
    # Add spaces
```

If this flag truly meant "spaces were already applied", then adding more spaces would be wrong. But if it means "we should apply spaces", then it makes sense.

Given the naming and the issue we're seeing, I believe the intention is that this flag should mean "spaces were already applied", so when it's `True`, we should NOT apply them again.

=== Testing Unordered List Case ===
Source markdown:
'   >    >    + list\n        >      item'
[The command completed with exit code 0.]
[Current working directory: /workspace/jackdewinter__pymarkdown__0.9]
[Python interpreter: /opt/conda/envs/testbed/bin/python]
[Command finished with exit code 0]</tool_response><|assistant|></think>I can see that the first line is correct in the final output: `'   >    >    1. list'`. So the first line works fine.

The issue is only with the second line. Let me trace this more carefully.

Actually, let me step back and understand the problem better. The container line comes from `split_container_text` which splits the original input:

Input: `'   >    >    1. list\n        >       item'`
Split: `['   >    >    1. list', '        >       item', '']`

So the second element `'        >       item'` already has the correct 8 spaces. But somehow it's getting converted to 16 spaces.

Let me look at what happens in the processing. The issue is that `__apply_primary_transformation` is adding spaces to a line that already has the correct spaces.

The key insight is in the flag name and meaning. Let me understand what `applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line` really means by looking at how it's calculated:

```python
applied_leading_spaces_to_start_of_container_line = not (
    did_move_ahead and current_changed_record[0]
)
```

Let me check what `did_move_ahead` and `current_changed_record[0]` represent in our case:

From the debug output:
- First line: `did_move_ahead>True`, `applied_leading_spaces flag = False` 
- Second line: `did_move_ahead>False`, `applied_leading_spaces flag = True`

So when `did_move_ahead` is `True`, the flag is `False`, meaning "we did NOT apply leading spaces".
When `did_move_ahead` is `False`, the flag is `True`, meaning "we DID apply leading spaces".

But this is counter-intuitive. Let me think about what `did_move_ahead` means.

Looking at the logic, `did_move_ahead` seems to indicate whether we moved to a new record. When we're on the first line of a container, we move ahead to new records. When we're on continuation lines, we don't move ahead.

So:
- First line: `did_move_ahead=True`, flag=`False` → We should apply spaces (and we do, correctly)
- Second line: `did_move_ahead=False`, flag=`True` → We should NOT apply spaces (but we do, incorrectly)

Therefore, the logic in `__apply_primary_transformation` IS backwards. When the flag is `True`, it means spaces were already applied, so we should NOT apply them again.

1. In the failing tests, we have cases where the container line already has the correct leading spaces
2. My fix was too broad - it prevented ALL duplicates, but some duplicates are legitimate (when they represent different semantic elements)

Let me look at the specific case that was failing. The key insight is that in our original problem case:

- Container line: `'        >       item'` (8 spaces + `>       item`)
- Leading space to add: `'        '` (8 spaces)
- Result after adding: `'                >       item'` (16 spaces + `>       item`)

The problem is that the container line already STARTS with the exact leading space we want to add.

But in the cases that broke, the container line doesn't start with the same leading space pattern.

Let me re-implement my fix, but make it more precise:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/jackdewinter__pymarkdown__0.9/test/transform_to_markdown.py</arg_value><arg_key>new_str</arg_key><arg_value>            if last_container_token_index < len(split_leading_spaces):
